Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ...

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...

!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!!

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields (arranged in a 3×3 grid)
2. The game has special rules:
   - First move: any cell can be chosen
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was in position (xl, yl) of some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If there are no empty cells in the required field, the player can choose any empty cell
3. We need to find all valid next moves based on the last move position

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines (9 lines with 3 groups of 3 characters each, 2 empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3×3 fields
- Read the last move coordinates (x, y) - note these are 1-indexed

### Step 2: Map coordinates to small fields
- Coordinates range from 1-9
- Position (x, y) belongs to field at row: (x-1)//3, column: (y-1)//3
- So if x=6, y=4: (6-1)//3 = 1, (4-1)//3 = 1 → field at row 1, col 1 (0-indexed)

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the field at the last move position has empty cells, the current player can only move in that field
- If that field has no empty cells, the current player can move anywhere
- Mark all valid positions with "!" instead of "."

### Step 4: Output the result

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: Last move at (6,4)
- 6→(5)//3=1, 4→(3)//3=1 → field (1,1) (bottom left field)
- The bottom left field (row 1, col 1) has "x" at position (5,2) 
- So we need to mark all empty cells in the bottom left field as "!"
- The bottom left field is rows 7-9, columns 1-3 (1-indexed)

```python
# Read input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    if line:  # Skip empty lines
        lines.append(line)

# Parse the 9 fields
fields = []
current_field = []
for i in range(9):
    if i % 3 == 0 and i > 0:
        fields.append(''.join(current_field))
        current_field = []
    current_field.append(lines[i].strip())
fields.append(''.join(current_field))

# Read last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed and find which field the last move was in
field_x = (x - 1) // 3  # 0-2
field_y = (y - 1) // 3  # 0-2

# Find the position within the field
pos_x = (x - 1) % 3
pos_y = (y - 1) % 3

# Determine valid moves
# If there are empty cells in the target field, only those cells are valid
# Otherwise, any empty cell is valid

# Identify which field to play in
target_field_x = field_x
target_field_y = field_y

# Get the target field
target_field = fields[target_field_x][target_field_y]

# Check if this field has any empty cells
# If it does, only positions in this field can be played
# If it doesn't, the player can play anywhere

# Find all empty cells in the target field
target_field_cells = []
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = target_field_x * 3 + i
        col = target_field_y * 3 + j
        if fields[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_cells.append((row, col))

# Check if there are any empty cells in the target field
has_empty_in_target = len(target_field_cells) > 0

# If there are empty cells in target field, only play in those positions
# Otherwise, play anywhere (all empty cells)

result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        cell = fields[i][j]
        if i == target_field_x and j == target_field_y:
            # This is the target field, we need to check if it has empty cells
            if has_empty_in_target:
                # Only if the cell was empty in the target field
                if fields[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(cell)
            else:
                # No empty cells in target field, so play anywhere
                if fields[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(cell)
        else:
            # Not in target field - can only play if it's empty
            if fields[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(cell)

```python
# Read input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    if line:
        lines.append(line)

# Extract the 9 fields (3x3 each)
fields = []
current_field = []
for i in range(9):
    if i % 3 == 0 and i > 0:
        fields.append(''.join(current_field))
        current_field = []
    current_field.append(lines[i].strip())
fields.append(''.join(current_field))

# Read last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed and find which field the last move was in
field_x = (x - 1) // 3  # 0-2
field_y = (y - 1) // 3  # 0-2

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
has_empty_in_target = False
target_field_empty_positions = []

for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = field_x * 3 + i
        col_idx = field_y * 3 + j
        if fields[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target = True
            target_field_empty_positions.append((row_idx, col_idx))

# Create result
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # If current cell is empty, check if it's in target field or if target field has no empty cells
        if fields[i][j] == '.':
            if has_empty_in_target:
                # Only if this cell is in the target field
                if i >= field_x * 3 and i < (field_x + 1) * 3 and j >= field_y * 3 and j < (field_y + 1) * 3:
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append('.')
            else:
                # No empty cells in target field, so any empty cell is valid
                row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(fields[i][j])
    result.append(row)

# Output result
for i in range(9):
    row_str = ""
    for j in range(9):
        row_str += result[i][j]
        if j < 8:
            row_str += " "
    print(row_str)

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 small 3x3 fields
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move: any cell in any field
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
   - If that field has no empty cells, the player can move to any empty cell
3. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of the 9x9 board (3 lines for each of 9 fields)
- Parse the coordinates of the last move (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine the current small field
- The board is divided into 9 fields: positions (1,1) to (3,3) where each field is 3x3
- Convert coordinates (x, y) to field indices:
  - Field row: (x-1) // 3
  - Field col: (y-1) // 3
- The last move was in field at (field_row, field_col)

### Step 3: Determine possible moves
- If the field at (field_row, field_col) has empty cells (contains '.'), then the current player must move to one of those cells
- If the field has no empty cells, the current player can move to any empty cell on any field
- For each empty cell in the corresponding field, mark it with '!' (but only if it's empty)

### Step 4: Output the result
- Print the board with '!' in valid positions, keeping original characters elsewhere

## Implementation

```python
# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# Find the position within the small field
field_x = x % 3
field_y = y % 3

# Determine the possible moves
# The current player must move in the field that contains the last move (unless that field is full)
possible_moves = []

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
target_field_start_row = field_row * 3
target_field_start_col = field_col * 3

# Check each cell in the target field
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = target_field_start_row + i
        col = target_field_start_col + j
        # Check if this cell is empty
        cell = board[row][col]
        if cell == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            possible_moves.append((row, col))

# If target field has empty cells, we can only move to those empty cells
# If target field has no empty cells, we can move to any empty cell
if target_field_has_empty:
    # Only mark empty cells in the target field
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row = target_field_start_row + i
            col = target_field_start_col + j
            if board[row][col] == '.':
                # Mark this cell with '!'
                # We need to reconstruct the board with only the target field modified
                pass
else:
    # Target field has no empty cells, so we can move to any empty cell
    # Mark all empty cells with '!'
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            row = i
            col = j
            if board[row][col] == '.':
                # Mark this cell with '!'
                pass

# Rebuild the output
output = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Check if this cell is in the target field
        if i >= field_row * 3 and i < (field_row + 1) * 3 and j >= field_col * 3 and j < (field_col + 1) * 3:
            # This cell is in the target field
            if board[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(board[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the target field
            row.append(board[i][j])
    output.append(''.join(row))

# Print the output with spaces between fields
for i in range(9):
    print(' '.join(output[i].split()))
```

# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# Find the position within the small field
field_x = x % 3
field_y = y % 3

# Determine the possible moves
# The current player must move in the field that contains the last move (unless that field is full)
target_field_has_empty = False
target_field_start_row = field_row * 3
target_field_start_col = field_col * 3

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = target_field_start_row + i
        col = target_field_start_col + j
        if board[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Rebuild the output
output = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Check if this cell is in the target field
        if i >= target_field_start_row and i < target_field_start_row + 3 and j >= target_field_start_col and j < target_field_start_col + 3:
            # This cell is in the target field
            if board[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(board[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the target field
            row.append(board[i][j])
    output.append(''.join(row))

# Print the output with spaces between fields
for i in range(9):
    print(' '.join(output[i].split()))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 



Input

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
5 5


Output

o!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

!!! xxx !!! 
!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!! 

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move: can be anywhere
   - After that: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If that field has no empty cells, the player can move anywhere

3. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player based on the last move position.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of 3 groups of 3 characters each (with spaces)
- Store the board state
- Read the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine which small field the last move was in
- The 9 small fields are:
  - (0,0): top-left, rows 0-2, cols 0-2
  - (0,1): top-middle, rows 0-2, cols 3-5
  - (0,2): top-right, rows 0-2, cols 6-8
  - (1,0): middle-left, rows 3-5, cols 0-2
  - (1,1): middle-center, rows 3-5, cols 3-5
  - (1,2): middle-right, rows 3-5, cols 6-8
  - (2,0): bottom-left, rows 6-8, cols 0-2
  - (2,1): bottom-middle, rows 6-8, cols 3-5
  - (2,2): bottom-right, rows 6-8, cols 6-8

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- Find which small field contains the last move
- If that field has empty cells, player can only move in that field
- If that field has no empty cells, player can move anywhere

### Step 4: Apply the changes
- Mark valid cells with "!" and leave others unchanged

## Implementation

```python
# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    # Split by spaces to get 3 groups of 3 characters
    groups = line.split()
    board.append(''.join(groups))

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Find the corresponding small field indices
small_field_x = field_x
small_field_y = field_y

# Determine valid moves
# Extract the small field where the last move was made
small_field = board[3 * small_field_x + small_field_y]

# Check if this small field has any empty cells
has_empty = False
for i in range(9):
    if small_field[i] == '.':
        has_empty = True
        break

# If the field has empty cells, current player can only move in that field
# If the field has no empty cells, current player can move anywhere

# Create result board
result = []
for i in range(9):
    line = []
    for j in range(9):
        if j % 3 == 0:
            line.append('')  # Start of new group
        if j % 3 == 2:  # End of group
            line.append('')  # Add empty space
            # Actually, we need to process by 3x3 fields
            pass

# Let me restructure this to be clearer

# Create result board
result_board = []
for i in range(9):
    result_board.append(list(board[i]))

# Determine which field the last move was in
field_x = (x - 1) // 3
field_y = (y - 1) // 3

# Check if the target field has empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
target_start_row = 3 * field_x
target_start_col = 3 * field_y

# Check all 9 cells in the target field
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        row_idx = target_start_row + r
        col_idx = target_start_col + c
        if board[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Apply the rules
if target_field_has_empty:
    # Player can only move in the target field
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            row_idx = target_start_row + r
            col_idx = target_start_col + c
            if board[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
                result_board[row_idx][col_idx] = '!'
else:
    # Player can move anywhere (since target field is full)
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board[i][j] == '.':
                result_board[i][j] = '!'

# Format output
for i in range(9):
    line = []
    for j in range(9):
        line.append(result_board[i][j])
    # Split every 3 characters
    groups = []
    for k in range(0, 9, 3):
        groups.append(''.join(line[k:k+3]))
    print(' '.join(groups))

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields (positions 1-9)
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move: can go to any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If that field has no empty cells, the player can go anywhere

3. We're given:
   - The current state of the 9×9 grid
   - Coordinates (x, y) of the last move (1-indexed)

4. We need to output the same grid but with "!" in cells where the current player can move

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines (9 lines of fields with spaces, 2 empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3×3 fields
- Parse the last move coordinates

### Step 2: Determine the constraints
- Convert (x, y) to 0-indexed
- Find which small field this corresponds to
- The small field index is (x-1)//3, (y-1)//3

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the target small field (from the last move) has empty cells, the current player can only move in that field
- If the target small field has no empty cells, the current player can move anywhere

### Step 4: Apply the rules to generate output
- For each cell in the 9×9 grid:
  - If the cell is in the target field from the last move and is empty → mark with "!"
  - If the target field has no empty cells → mark all empty cells with "!"
  - Otherwise, keep original character

### Step 5: Format output
- Print the grid in the same format

# For field at (field_row, field_col), it occupies rows 3*field_row to 3*field_row+2, and columns 3*field_col to 3*field_col+2

# For field at column j in this line, it's at field_col = j

# - field_row = i // 3
# - parts = line.split() - has 3 parts (fields at col 0,1,2)
# - for j in 0,1,2: field at (field_row, j) has 3x3 characters

# For line i, j in 0,1,2:
#   for k in 0,1,2: 
#       row = 3 * (i // 3) + k
#       col = 3 * j + k
#       grid[row][col] = field_chars[k]

for _ in range(9):
    lines.append(input().strip())

grid = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

for i in range(9):
    parts = lines[i].split()
    field_row = i // 3
    for j in range(3):
        field_chars = parts[j]
        for k in range(3):
            row = 3 * field_row + k
            col = 3 * j + k
            grid[row][col] = field_chars[k]

# Read coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3  # which field row
field_y = y // 3  # which field column

# Determine the target field (where the next move should go)
# If the target field has any empty cells, then the current player can only move in that field
# If the target field has no empty cells, then the current player can move anywhere

# Find if the target field has empty cells
target_has_empty = False
target_field_rows = 3 * field_x
target_field_cols = 3 * field_y

# Go through each cell in the target field
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        row = target_field_rows + r
        col = target_field_cols + c
        if grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_has_empty:
        break

# Now determine where we can place "!" 
# If target field has empty cells, we can only place in that field and only in empty cells
# If target field has no empty cells, we can place in any empty cell

# Build the result grid
result = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

# Copy original grid
for i in range(9):
    for j in range(9):
        result[i][j] = grid[i][j]

# Apply the rules
if target_has_empty:
    # Can only move in the target field in empty cells
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            row = target_field_rows + r
            col = target_field_cols + c
            if grid[row][col] == '.':
                result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # Can move anywhere in empty cells
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Output the result in the same format
for i in range(9):
    line = ""
    for j in range(9):
        if j % 3 == 0:
            line += " "
        line += result[i][j]
    print(line.strip())

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(9):
    lines.append(input().strip())

# Parse coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Build 9x9 grid from input
grid = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

for i in range(9):
    parts = lines[i].split()
    field_row = i // 3
    for j in range(3):
        field_chars = parts[j]
        for k in range(3):
            row = 3 * field_row + k
            col = 3 * j + k
            grid[row][col] = field_chars[k]

# Find the field where the last move was made
x -= 1  # convert to 0-indexed
y -= 1  # convert to 0-indexed

field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
target_has_empty = False
target_start_row = 3 * field_x
target_start_col = 3 * field_y

for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        row = target_start_row + r
        col = target_start_col + c
        if grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_has_empty:
        break

# Create result grid
result = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

# Copy original and apply "!" where needed
for i in range(9):
    for j in range(9):
        result[i][j] = grid[i][j]

if target_has_empty:
    # Only in the target field's empty cells
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            row = target_start_row + r
            col = target_start_col + c
            if grid[row][col] == '.':
                result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # Any empty cell
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Format output: each row is 3 fields separated by space
for i in range(9):
    row_parts = []
    for j in range(9):
        if j % 3 == 0:
            row_parts.append("")
        row_parts.append(result[i][j])
    
    # Combine with spaces between the 3-character parts
    line = " ".join(row_parts)
    print(line)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 



Input

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
5 5


Output

o!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

!!! xxx !!! 
!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!! 

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

6. We need to output the possible next moves with "!" for valid positions, otherwise keep the original board

- The current player must move in that specific small field (or can move anywhere if no empty cells)

### Step 3: Apply the rules
- If the target small field has empty cells, mark those with "!"
- If the target small field has no empty cells, mark all empty cells in the entire board with "!"

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the board
board = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x0 = x - 1
y0 = y - 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = x0 // 3
field_col = y0 // 3

# Find the small field coordinates (field_row, field_col)
# The small fields are 3x3: (0,0) to (2,2)

# Create a copy of the board to modify
result = [list(row) for row in board]

# Find all empty cells in the small field at (field_row, field_col)
field_cells = []
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_row * 3 + i
        col = field_col * 3 + j
        field_cells.append((row, col))

# Check if there are any empty cells in that field
has_empty = False
for row, col in field_cells:
    if result[row][col] == '.':
        has_empty = True
        break

# If there are empty cells in the target field, only mark those with "!"
# If no empty cells, mark all empty cells anywhere with "!"
if has_empty:
    for row, col in field_cells:
        if result[row][col] == '.':
            result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # Mark all empty cells in the entire board with "!"
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if result[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(' '.join(row))
```

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields (positions 1-9)
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move: player can place chip in any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If no cells are available in that required field, the player can place in any empty cell

3. We're given the current state of the game and the coordinates of the last move
4. We need to output all possible next moves with "!" in those positions

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines (9 with fields and 2 empty lines between)
- Extract the 9×9 grid
- Parse the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Map coordinates to field positions
- The 9×9 grid has 9 fields: (1,1) to (3,3) where each field is 3×3
- For coordinates (x,y): 
  - Field row = (x-1) // 3
  - Field col = (y-1) // 3
  - Within field: row = (x-1) % 3, col = (y-1) % 3

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the last move was at position (xl, yl), the next move must be in the field at (xl, yl)
- If there are no empty cells in that field, any empty cell is valid
- Otherwise, only cells in that specific field are valid

### Step 4: Output the result
- Go through each cell and if it's valid, put "!" instead of "."

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: Last move at (6,4)
- Field row = (6-1)//3 = 1, Field col = (4-1)//3 = 0
- So we need moves in field (1,0) = lower left field
- This field has a chip at (2,0) in 0-based indexing, so we mark all empty cells in that field with "!"

**Example 2**: Last move at (7,4)
- Field row = (7-1)//3 = 2, Field col = (4-1)//3 = 0
- So we need moves in field (2,0) = lower left field
- But all cells in this field are occupied, so we mark all empty cells globally with "!"

**Example 3**: Last move at (5,5)
- Field row = (5-1)//3 = 1, Field col = (5-1)//3 = 1
- So we need moves in field (1,1) = center field
- All cells in center field are occupied, so we can move anywhere → mark all empty cells with "!"

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    if line:
        lines.append(line)

# Parse the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# The next move must be in the field at (field_row, field_col)
# But if that field has no empty cells, any empty cell is valid

# Convert the 9x9 grid into a 2D array
grid = []
for i in range(9):
    row = lines[i].split('   ')  # Split by 3 spaces
    grid.append(row)

# Find if the target field has any empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
target_field_row = field_row
target_field_col = field_col

# Check if there are any empty cells in the target field
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        row_idx = target_field_row * 3 + r
        col_idx = target_field_col * 3 + c
        if grid[row_idx][c] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Build the result
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if grid[i][j] == '.':
            if target_field_has_empty:
                # If target field has empty cells, we can only move in that field
                field_r = i // 3
                field_c = j // 3
                if field_r == target_field_row and field_c == target_field_col:
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append('.')
            else:
                # If target field has no empty cells, we can move anywhere
                row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(grid[i][j])
    result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4

... x.. ...

!!! ... ...

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields (arranged in a 3x3 layout)
2. The game has specific movement rules:
   - First move: any cell in any field
   - After that: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
   - If that field has no empty cells, the player can move to any empty cell

3. We need to:
   - Parse the 9x9 grid with 9 fields
   - Given the last move coordinates (x, y), determine all possible next moves
   - Output the grid with "!" in positions where the current player can move

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of input (9 for the grid, 1 for coordinates)
- Split the grid into 9 fields of 3x3 each
- Convert coordinates from 1-based to 0-based indexing

### Step 2: Determine which field is affected
- The last move was at position (x, y)
- Find which small field this position belongs to
- Small fields are arranged as:
  - Top row: fields (0,0), (0,1), (0,2) - rows 0-2, cols 0-2, 3-5, 6-8
  - Middle row: fields (1,0), (1,1), (1,2)
  - Bottom row: fields (2,0), (2,1), (2,2)

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the target field has empty cells, player must move in that field
- Otherwise, player can move in any empty cell

### Step 4: Output the result
- Replace empty cells (".") with "!" where valid moves exist
- Keep original characters ("x", "o") in occupied cells
- Maintain the same formatting

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the grid - 9x9 total, divided into 9 fields of 3x3
grid = []
for i in range(9):
    row = lines[i].split(' ')
    grid.append(row)

# Extract the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, lines[9].split())
# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (which row of small fields)
field_y = y // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (which column of small fields)

# Find the "forced" field - the field that the current player must move into
forced_field_x = field_x
forced_field_y = field_y

# Determine the valid positions
valid_positions = []

# First, check if the forced field has any empty cells
forced_field_start_row = forced_field_x * 3
forced_field_start_col = forced_field_y * 3

has_empty_in_forced_field = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = forced_field_start_row + i
        col_idx = forced_field_start_col + j
        if grid[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            has_empty_in_forced_field = True
            # Mark this position as valid
            valid_positions.append((row_idx, col_idx))

# If there are no empty cells in the forced field, the player can move anywhere
if not has_empty_in_forced_field:
    # Add all empty cells from the entire grid
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                valid_positions.append((i, j))
else:
    # Otherwise, only the empty cells in the forced field are valid
    pass

# Create the output grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if (i, j) in valid_positions:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(grid[i][j])
    result.append(row)

# Format and output the result
output_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    row_str = ''
    for j in range(9):
        row_str += result[i][j]
        if j < 8:
            row_str += ' '
    output_lines.append(row_str)

# Output with spacing (every 3 columns with space)
for line in output_lines:
    print(line)
```

# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the grid - the input has 9 rows of 3 parts each, separated by spaces
grid = []
for i in range(9):
    # Split the line into 3 parts and then join them back
    row_parts = lines[i].split(' ')
    row = []
    for part in row_parts:
        row.extend(list(part))
    grid.append(row)

# Extract the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, lines[9].split())
# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (which row of small fields)
field_y = y // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (which column of small fields)

# Find the "forced" field - the field that the current player must move into
forced_field_start_row = field_x * 3
forced_field_start_col = field_y * 3

# Determine valid positions
valid_positions = []

# Check if the forced field has any empty cells
has_empty_in_forced_field = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = forced_field_start_row + i
        col_idx = forced_field_start_col + j
        if grid[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            has_empty_in_forced_field = True
            valid_positions.append((row_idx, col_idx))

# If there are no empty cells in the forced field, the player can move anywhere
if not has_empty_in_forced_field:
    # Add all empty cells from the entire grid
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                valid_positions.append((i, j))
else:
    # Otherwise, only the empty cells in the forced field are valid
    pass

# Create the output grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if (i, j) in valid_positions:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(grid[i][j])
    result.append(row)

# Format and output the result
for i in range(9):
    row_str = ''
    for j in range(9):
        row_str += result[i][j]
        if j < 8:
            row_str += ' '
    print(row_str)

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. Players take turns placing chips (x or o)
3. After the first move, there are restrictions: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
4. If that small field has no empty cells, the player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to find all valid next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the board (with spaces and empty lines)
- Extract each 3×3 small field
- Get the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine the small field of the last move
- The board has 9 small fields arranged in a 3×3 grid
- A position (x, y) in the 9×9 board belongs to small field at:
  - row_field = (x-1) // 3
  - col_field = (y-1) // 3

### Step 3: Determine valid next moves
- If the small field where the last move was made has empty cells, the current player must play in that field
- If that field has no empty cells, the current player can play in any empty cell

### Step 4: Output the result
- Replace empty cells (".") with "!" in valid positions
- Keep other cells unchanged

## Implementation

```python
# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# Find the small field that needs to be used for the next move
# The next move must be in the small field at (field_row, field_col)
next_field_row = field_row
next_field_col = field_col

# Convert the board into a 3x3 grid of 3x3 fields
# Each field is 3x3, so we need to access cells in the appropriate range
fields = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        row.append(board[i][j])
    fields.append(row)

# Find all empty cells in the target field
target_field_row = field_row
target_field_col = field_col

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
has_empty_in_target = False
target_cells = []

# Find all empty cells in the target field
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = target_field_row * 3 + i
        col_idx = target_field_col * 3 + j
        if fields[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target = True
            target_cells.append((row_idx, col_idx))

# Determine valid moves
valid_moves = []

if has_empty_in_target:
    # The current player must play in the target field
    for row, col in target_cells:
        valid_moves.append((row, col))
else:
    # The target field has no empty cells, so current player can play anywhere
    # Find all empty cells in the entire board
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if fields[i][j] == '.':
                valid_moves.append((i, j))

# Output the result
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if (i, j) in valid_moves:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(board[i][j])
    print(' '.join(row))
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the field indexing. Let me reconsider the problem.

The last move was at coordinates (x,y) which belongs to a small field. The next move must be in the small field that has the same coordinates (x//3, y//3).

Let me fix the solution:

```python
# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# Find all empty cells in the target field
target_cells = []

# The next move must be in the small field at (field_row, field_col)
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = field_row * 3 + i
        col_idx = field_col * 3 + j
        if board[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            target_cells.append((row_idx, col_idx))

# If there are empty cells in the target field, that's where we can move
# Otherwise, we can move anywhere in the board
valid_moves = []

if target_cells:
    # Can only move in the target field
    valid_moves = target_cells
else:
    # Can move anywhere in the board
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board[i][j] == '.':
                valid_moves.append((i, j))

# Output the result
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if (i, j) in valid_moves:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(board[i][j])
    print(' '.join(row))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0. 

And there is no sofa with the set (1, 0, 0, 0) so the answer is -1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m grid
2. Each sofa occupies 2 adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. "to the left" means there exists a cell in sofa A with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in sofa B
5. "to the top" means there exists a cell in sofa A with smaller y-coordinate than a cell in sofa B

## Key Insights

1. For each sofa, we need to determine how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, and bottom
2. A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of some cell in B
3. This means we need to check the minimum and maximum coordinates of each sofa

## Algorithm

1. For each sofa, find the range of x-coordinates and y-coordinates that it occupies
2. For each sofa, count:
   - Sofas to its left: sofas where min_x < current_sofa's min_x OR where max_x < current_sofa's min_x
   - Actually, it's simpler: a sofa B is to the left of sofa A if there exists a cell in B with x < cell in A
   - So sofa B is to the left of sofa A if min_x_B < max_x_A (because if B has any cell with x < max_x_A, and A has a cell with x >= min_x_B, then there's overlap)
   - More precisely: sofa B is to the left of sofa A if min_x_B < max_x_A AND there exists a position where they can "see" each other

Wait, let me reconsider:

A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there exists a cell a in A and a cell b in B such that x_a < x_b.

This means:
- For sofa A to have sofas to its left: there must be some cell in A with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of some cell in other sofas
- Specifically, if a sofa has min_x_A, then any sofa that has max_x_B > min_x_A and has at least one cell with x > min_x_A will have sofas to the left of it

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me think simpler:

For a given sofa S, a different sofa T is to the left of S if there exists a cell in T and a cell in S such that x_t < x_s.

This happens if min_x_T < max_x_S and min_x_T < max_x_S and there's overlap in the x-direction.

Actually, it's simpler: T is to the left of S if max_x_T > min_x_S (because if T's rightmost cell is to the right of S's leftmost cell, then there's a cell in T with x > some cell in S).

Wait, that's still not quite right.

Let me re-express: 
- T is to the left of S if there exists some cell t in T and some cell s in S such that x_t < x_s.
- This happens if min_x_T < max_x_S (since if min_x_T < max_x_S, then the leftmost cell of T is to the left of the rightmost cell of S, so we can find a cell in T with x < some x in S).

Actually, no: if min_x_T < max_x_S, it doesn't guarantee that x_t < x_s for some x_s in S. We need min_x_T < x_s for some x_s in S.

The condition is: T is to the left of S if min_x_T < max_x_S.

Similarly:
- T is to the right of S if min_x_S < max_x_T
- T is to the top of S if min_y_T < max_y_S  
- T is to the bottom of S if min_y_S < max_y_T

Wait, that's still not quite right. Let me think again:

T is to the left of S means: exists t in T, s in S such that x_t < x_s.
This means min_x_T < max_x_S (because if min_x_T < max_x_S, then we can find a cell in T (the one with min_x_T) that has x < some cell in S (any cell with x > min_x_T)).

Similarly:
- T is to the right of S if min_x_S < max_x_T
- T is to the top of S if min_y_T < max_y_S
- T is to the bottom of S if min_y_S < max_y_T

Yes, this seems correct.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each sofa, calculate its min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y
2. For each sofa i, iterate through all other sofas j
3. For each j, check if j is to the left of i, right of i, top of i, bottom of i using the conditions above
4. Count these and compare with given cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b
5. Return the sofa number (1-indexed) that matches, or -1 if none

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Calculate min and max for x and y coordinates
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, calculate how many sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
result = -1

for i in range(d):
    left = 0
    right = 0
    top = 0
    bottom = 0
    
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
            
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        
        # Check if j is to the left of i
        # j is to the left of i if there exists cell in j with x < cell in i
        if min_x_j < max_x_i:
            left += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the right of i  
        # j is to the right of i if there exists cell in j with x > cell in i
        if min_x_i < max_x_j:
            right += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the top of i
        # j is to the top of i if there exists cell in j with y < cell in i
        if min_y_j < max_y_i:
            top += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the bottom of i
        # j is to the bottom of i if there exists cell in j with y > cell in i
        if min_y_i < max_y_j:
            bottom += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
    if left == cnt_l and right == cnt_r and top == cnt_t and bottom == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1  # 1-indexed
        break

print(result)
```

Wait, I think there's a logical error in my condition. Let me reconsider:

For sofa j to be to the left of sofa i:
- We need a cell in j with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of some cell in i
- This happens if min_x_j < max_x_i (because if min_x_j < max_x_i, then the leftmost cell of j has x < some x in i)

Similarly:
- j is to the right of i if min_x_i < max_x_j
- j is to the top of i if min_y_j < max_y_i  
- j is to the bottom of i if min_y_i < max_y_j

This seems logically sound.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1:
- Sofa 1: (3,1)-(3,2) → min_x=3, max_x=3, min_y=1, max_y=2
- Sofa 2: (1,2)-(2,2) → min_x=1, max_x=2, min_y=2, max_y=2
- Count for sofa 1:
  - Left: sofa 2 has min_x=1 < max_x=3 → left=1 ✓
  - Right: sofa 2 has min_x=1 < max_x=3 → no, wait: min_x_i=3, max_x_j=2 → 3 < 2? false → right=0 ✓
  - Top: sofa 2 has min_y=2 < max_y=2? false → top=0 ✓
  - Bottom: sofa 2 has min_y=2 < max_y=2? false → bottom=0 ✓
  But we need left=1, right=0, top=0, bottom=1? 

Wait, looking at the input: cnt_l=1, cnt_r=0, cnt_t=0, cnt_b=1.

For sofa 1:
- left: any sofa j with min_x_j < max_x_1=3 → sofa 2 has min_x=1 < 3 → left=1 ✓
- right: any sofa j with min_x_1=3 < max_x_j → 3 < max_x_j → no sofa has max_x_j > 3 → right=0 ✓  
- top: any sofa j with min_y_j < max_y_1=2 → sofa 2 has min_y=2 < 2? false → top=0 ✓
- bottom: any sofa j with min_y_1=1 < max_y_j → sofa 2 has min_y=2 > 1, so 1 < 2 → true → bottom=1 ✓

Perfect!

```python

            right += 1

        if min_y_j < max_y_i:
            top += 1

        if min_y_i < max_y_j:
            bottom += 1

## Implementation

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Store min and max coordinates for each sofa
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# Check each sofa
for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    left_count = 0  # sofas j where min_x(j) < max_x(i)
    right_count = 0  # sofas j where max_x(j) > min_x(i)
    top_count = 0  # sofas j where min_y(j) < max_y(i)
    bottom_count = 0  # sofas j where max_y(j) > min_y(i)
    
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
            
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        
        # Sofa j is to the left of sofa i if min_x(j) < max_x(i)
        if min_x_j < max_x_i:
            left_count += 1
            
        # Sofa j is to the right of sofa i if max_x(j) > min_x(i)
        if max_x_j > min_x_i:
            right_count += 1
            
        # Sofa j is to the top of sofa i if min_y(j) < max_y(i)
        if min_y_j < max_y_i:
            top_count += 1
            
        # Sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i if max_y(j) > min_y(i)
        if max_y_j > min_y_i:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        print(i + 1)
        exit()

print(-1)
```

Wait, this will be O(d²) which might be too slow for d up to 10⁵. Let me optimize.

Actually, d ≤ 10⁵, so O(d²) would be 10¹⁰ operations which is too slow in Python.

I need to optimize by precomputing coordinates.

Let me check the constraints again: d ≤ 10⁵, n, m ≤ 10⁵.

For each sofa, I need to count how many sofas have:
- min_x < max_x_i (for left)
- max_x > min_x_i (for right)
- min_y < max_y_i (for top)
- max_y > min_y_i (for bottom)

I can precompute all min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y values and then sort them.

But the counting is independent for each dimension.

For left count: for sofa i, count number of sofas j where min_x_j < max_x_i
This is equivalent to: count of sofas with min_x_j < some value.

So I can:
1. Precompute all min_x values
2. Sort them
3. For each sofa i, use binary search to find how many min_x values are less than max_x_i

Similarly for other dimensions.

Let me implement the optimized version:

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
min_x_vals = []
max_x_vals = []
min_y_vals = []
max_y_vals = []

for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))
    min_x_vals.append(min_x)
    max_x_vals.append(max_x)
    min_y_vals.append(min_y)
    max_y_vals.append(max_y)

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# Sort the arrays for binary search
min_x_vals.sort()
max_x_vals.sort()
min_y_vals.sort()
max_y_vals.sort()

# Function to count number of elements less than target
def count_less(arr, target):
    # Binary search for first position >= target
    left, right = 0, len(arr)
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if arr[mid] < target:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    return left

# Check each sofa
for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    left_count = count_less(min_x_vals, max_x_i)
    # Right count: max_x_j > min_x_i
    right_count = d - count_less(max_x_vals, min_x_i)
    
    top_count = count_less(min_y_vals, max_y_i)
    bottom_count = d - count_less(max_y_vals, min_y_i)
    
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        print(i + 1)
        exit()

print(-1)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2
